The admissible monomial bases for the polynomial algebra of five variables in some types of generic degrees
Nguyễn Sum

Abstract
. Let P k P_{k} be the graded polynomial algebra 𝔽 2 ​ [ x 1 , x 2 , … , x k ] \mathbb{F}_{2}[x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{k}] over the prime field of two elements, 𝔽 2 \mathbb{F}_{2} , with the degree of each x i x_{i} being 1. We study the hit problem , set up by Frank Peterson, of finding a minimal set of generators for P k P_{k} as a module over the mod- 2 2 Steenrod algebra, 𝒜 . \mathcal{A}. In this paper, we explicitly determine a minimal set of 𝒜 \mathcal{A} -generators for P 5 P_{5} in the case of the generic degrees n = 2 d + 1 − 1 n=2^{d+1}-1 and n = 2 d + 1 − 2 n=2^{d+1}-2 for all d ⩾ 6 d\geqslant 6 .

1
1
footnotetext:
2000
Mathematics Subject Classification
. Primary 55S10; 55S05.
2
2
footnotetext:
Keywords and phrases:
Steenrod squares, Peterson hit problem, polynomial algebra, modular representation.

1.
Introduction and statement of main results

Let
E^{k}
be an elementary abelian 2-group of rank
k
and let
BE^{k}
be the classifying space of
E^{k}
. Then,

P_{k}:=H^{*}(BE^{k})\cong\mathbb{F}_{2}[x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{k}],

a polynomial algebra in
k
generators
x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{k}
, each of degree 1. Here the cohomology is taken with coefficients in the prime field
\mathbb{F}_{2}
of two elements. The algebra
P_{k}
is a module over the mod-2 Steenrod algebra,
\mathcal{A}
. The action of
\mathcal{A}
on
P_{k}
is determined by the elementary properties of the Steenrod squares
Sq^{i}
and subject to the Cartan formula (see Steenrod and Epstein
[
21
]
).

An element
g
in
P_{k}
is called
hit
if it belongs to
\mathcal{A}^{+}P_{k}
, where
\mathcal{A}^{+}
is the augmentation ideal of
\mathcal{A}
. That means
g
can be written as a finite sum
g=\sum_{u\geqslant 0}Sq^{2^{u}}(g_{u})
for suitable polynomials
g_{u}\in P_{k}
.

We study the
Peterson hit problem
of determining a minimal set of generators for the polynomial algebra
P_{k}
as a module over the Steenrod algebra. In other words, we want to determine a basis of the
\mathbb{F}_{2}
-vector space

QP_{k}:=P_{k}/\mathcal{A}^{+}P_{k}=\mathbb{F}_{2}\otimes_{\mathcal{A}}P_{k}.

This problem was first studied by Peterson
[
8
]
, Wood
[
32
]
, Singer
[
19
]
, and Priddy
[
14
]
, who showed its relation to several classical problems in Algebraic Topology. Then, this problem was investigated by Carlisle-Wood
[
1
]
, Crabb-Hubbuck
[
2
]
, Janfada-Wood
[
3
]
, Kameko
[
4
]
, Mothebe
[
6
]
, Nam
[
7
]
, Repka-Selick
[
15
]
, Silverman
[
16
,
17
]
, Silverman-Singer
[
18
]
, Singer
[
20
]
, Sum-Tín
[
27
]
, Walker and Wood
[
29
]
, Wood
[
33
]
, the present author
[
22
,
24
]
and others.

The vector space
QP_{k}
was explicitly computed by
Peterson
[
8
]
for
k=1,2,
by Kameko
[
4
]
for
k=3
and by us
[
24
]
for
k=4
. For
k>4
, this problem is still open.
Recently, the hit problem and its applications to representations of general linear groups have been presented in the monographs of Walker and Wood
[
30
,
31
]
.

For any nonnegative integer
n
, set
\mu(n)=\min\{m\in\mathbb{Z}:\alpha(n+m)\leqslant m\}
where
\alpha(a)
denotes the number of one in dyadic expansion of a positive integer
a
.
Denote by
(P_{k})_{n}
and
(QP_{k})_{n}
the subspaces of degree
n
homogeneous polynomials in the spaces
P_{k}
and
QP_{k}
respectively. The following is Peterson’s conjecture, which was established by Wood.

Theorem 1.1

(See Wood
[
32
]
)
.

If
\mu(n)>k
, then
(QP_{k})_{n}=0
.

One of the important tools in the study of the hit problem is Kameko’s homomorphism

\widetilde{Sq}^{0}_{*}:QP_{k}\to QP_{k}
.
This homomorphism is induced by the
\mathbb{F}_{2}
-linear map
\phi:P_{k}\to P_{k}
given by

\phi(x)=\begin{cases}y,&\text{if }x=x_{1}x_{2}\ldots x_{k}y^{2},\\
0,&\text{otherwise,}\end{cases}

for any monomial
x\in P_{k}
. Note that
\phi
is not an
\mathcal{A}
-homomorphism. However,

\phi Sq^{2t}=Sq^{t}\phi,
and
\phi Sq^{2t+1}=0
for any non-negative integer
t
.

Theorem 1.2

(See Kameko
[
4
]
)
.

Let
m
be a positive integer. If
\mu(2m+k)=k
, then

(\widetilde{Sq}^{0}_{*})_{(k,m)}:=\widetilde{Sq}^{0}_{*}:(QP_{k})_{2m+k}\longrightarrow(QP_{k})_{m}

is an isomorphism of the
\mathbb{F}_{2}
-vector spaces.

From Theorems
1.1
and
1.2
, the hit problem is reduced to the case of degree
n
such that
\mu(n)<k
. For
\mu(n)=k-1
, the problem was partially studied by Crabb-Hubbuck
[
2
]
, Nam
[
7
]
, Repka-Selick
[
15
]
, Walker-Wood
[
29
]
and the present author
[
22
,
24
]
. Recently, many authors study this problem for the case
k=5
.

To classify the situations of degrees, one can use the function
\beta(n)=\alpha(n+\mu(n))
. For
k=5
and
\beta(n)=1
, one gets
n=2^{d+1}-s
with
s=\mu(n)<5
, and the problem has been respectively determined by Phúc
[
9
]
and Phúc-Sum
[
13
]
for
s=3,\,4
.

To complete the solution of hit problem for
k=5
and the degrees
n
with
\beta(n)=1
, in the present paper, we explicitly determine all the admissible monomials (see Section
2
) of
P_{5}
in the generic degrees
n=2^{d+1}-s
with
s=1,\,2
and
d\geqslant 6
.

Theorem 1.3
.

For any integer
d\geqslant 6
, there exist exactly
1085
admissible monomials of degree
2^{d+1}-2
in
P_{5}
. Consequently
\dim(QP_{5})_{(2^{d+1}-2)}=1085.

For the cases
d=1,\,2
, the computation is easy and we have
\dim(QP_{5})_{2}=10
,
\dim(QP_{5})_{6}=74.
The cases
d=3,\,4
are respectively determined by Ly-Tín
[
5
]
and Sum-Mong
[
26
]
, they showed that
\dim(QP_{5})_{14}=320
and
\dim(QP_{5})_{30}=840.
For the case
d=5
, the computation is extremely complicated. However, we also show in Subsection
5.4
that
1105\leqslant\dim(QP_{5})_{62}\leqslant 1175.

For
n=2^{d+1}-1
, the space
(QP_{5})_{n}
has been determined with
d\leqslant 4
by Tín
[
28
]
, Phúc
[
11
]
and the present author
[
23
]
. For
d\geqslant 5
, we consider Kameko’s homomorphism

(\widetilde{Sq}^{0}_{*})_{(5,2^{d}-3)}:(QP_{5})_{(2^{d+1}-1)}\longrightarrow(QP_{5})_{(2^{d}-3)}.

This is an epimorphism and the space
(QP_{k})_{(2^{d}-3)}
has been computed in Phúc
[
9
]
. So, we need only to determine
\mbox{\rm Ker}\big((\widetilde{Sq}^{0}_{*})_{(5,2^{d}-3)}\big)
.

Theorem 1.4
.

For any integer
d\geqslant 6
, there exist exactly
496
classes of degree
2^{d+1}-1
in
\mbox{\rm Ker}\big((\widetilde{Sq}^{0}_{*})_{(5,2^{d}-3)}\big)
represented by the admissible monomials in
P_{5}
. Consequently

\dim\mbox{\rm Ker}\big((\widetilde{Sq}^{0}_{*})_{(5,2^{d}-3)}\big)=496.

Combining Theorem
1.4
and the results in Phúc
[
9
]
, we obtain the following.

Corollary 1.5
.

For any integer
d\geqslant 6
, we have

\dim(QP_{5})_{(2^{d+1}-1)}=\begin{cases}1441,\mbox{ if }d=6,\\
1611,\mbox{ if }d=7,\\
1612,\mbox{ if }d\geqslant 8.\end{cases}

The case
d=1
is easy and
\dim(QP_{5})_{3}=25
. The cases
d=2,\,3,\,4
were respectively determined in Tín
[
28
]
, Sum
[
23
]
, Phúc
[
11
]
, they showed that
\dim(QP_{5})_{7}=110
,
\dim(QP_{5})_{15}=432
and
\dim(QP_{5})_{31}=866.
For
d=5
, the computation of
(QP_{5})_{63}
is complicated. However, we also show in Subsection
5.5
that
1141\leqslant\dim(QP_{5})_{63}\leqslant 1155.

The proofs of Theorems
1.3
and
1.4
are very complicated. We prove by combining hand computation with the aids of some simple computer programmes.

The paper is organized as follows. In Section
2
, we recall some needed information on the admissible monomials in
P_{k}
, the criterions of Singer and Silverman on the hit monomials. The detailed proofs of Theorems
1.3
and
1.4
are respectively presented in Sections
3
and
4
. Finally, in Section
5
we list the needed admissible monomials of degrees
2^{d+1}-1
and
2^{d+1}-2
in
P_{5}
.

2.
Preliminaries

In this section, we recall some needed information from Kameko
[
4
]
, Singer
[
20
]
and the first named author
[
24
]
which will be used in the next section.

Notation 2.1
.

We denote
\mathbb{N}_{k}=\{1,2,\ldots,k\}
and

\displaystyle X_{\mathbb{J}}=X_{\{j_{1},j_{2},\ldots,j_{s}\}}=\prod_{j\in\mathbb{N}_{k}\setminus\mathbb{J}}x_{j},\ \ \mathbb{J}=\{j_{1},j_{2},\ldots,j_{s}\}\subset\mathbb{N}_{k},

In particular,
X_{\mathbb{N}_{k}}=1,\ X_{\emptyset}=x_{1}x_{2}\ldots x_{k},

X_{j}=x_{1}\ldots\hat{x}_{j}\ldots x_{k},\ 1\leqslant j\leqslant k,
and
X:=X_{k}\in P_{k-1}.

Let
\alpha_{i}(a)
denote the
i
-th coefficient in dyadic expansion of a non-negative integer
a
. That means

a=\alpha_{0}(a)2^{0}+\alpha_{1}(a)2^{1}+\alpha_{2}(a)2^{2}+\ldots,
for
\alpha_{i}(a)=0
or 1 with
i\geqslant 0
.

Let
x=x_{1}^{a_{1}}x_{2}^{a_{2}}\ldots x_{k}^{a_{k}}\in P_{k}
. Denote
\nu_{j}(x)=a_{j},1\leqslant j\leqslant k
and
\nu(x)=\max\{\nu_{j}(x):1\leqslant j\leqslant k\}
.
Set

\mathbb{J}_{t}(x)=\{j\in\mathbb{N}_{k}:\alpha_{t}(\nu_{j}(x))=0\},

for
t\geqslant 0
. Then, we have

x=\prod_{t\geqslant 0}X_{\mathbb{J}_{t}(x)}^{2^{t}}.

Definition 2.2
.

A weight vector
\omega
is a sequence of non-negative integers
(\omega_{1},\omega_{2},\ldots
,
\omega_{i},\ldots)
such that
\omega_{i}=0
for
i\gg 0
.

For a monomial
x
in
P_{k}
, define two sequences associated with
x
by

\displaystyle\omega(x)

\displaystyle=(\omega_{1}(x),\omega_{2}(x),\ldots,\omega_{i}(x),\ldots),

\displaystyle\sigma(x)

\displaystyle=(\nu_{1}(x),\nu_{2}(x),\ldots,\nu_{k}(x)),

where

\omega_{i}(x)=\sum_{1\leqslant j\leqslant k}\alpha_{i-1}(\nu_{j}(x))=\deg X_{\mathbb{J}_{i-1}(x)},\ i\geqslant 1.

The sequences
\omega(x)
and
\sigma(x)
are respectively called the weight vector and the exponent vector of
x
.

The sets of all the weight vectors and the exponent vectors are given the left lexicographical order.

For any weight vector
\omega=(\omega_{1},\omega_{2},\ldots)
, we define
\deg\omega=\sum_{i>0}2^{i-1}\omega_{i}
and the length
\ell(\omega)=\max\{i:\omega_{i}>0\}
. We write
\omega=(\omega_{1},\omega_{2},\ldots,\omega_{r})
if
\ell(\omega)=r
. For a weight vector
\eta=(\eta_{1},\eta_{2},\ldots)
, we define the concatenation of weight vectors

\omega|\eta=(\omega_{1},\ldots,\omega_{r},\eta_{1},\eta_{2},\ldots)

if
\ell(\omega)=r
and
(a)|^{b}=(a)|(a)|\ldots|(a)
, (
b
times of
(a)
’s), where
a,\,b
are positive integers.
Denote by
P_{k}(\omega)
the subspace of
P_{k}
spanned by monomials
y
such that

\deg y=\deg\omega
and
\omega(y)\leqslant\omega
, and by
P_{k}^{-}(\omega)
the subspace of
P_{k}(\omega)
spanned by monomials
y
such that
\omega(y)<\omega
.

Definition 2.3
.

Let
\omega
be a weight vector and
f,g
two polynomials of the same degree in
P_{k}
.

i)
f\equiv g
if and only if
f-g\in\mathcal{A}^{+}P_{k}
. If
f\equiv 0
then
f
is called
hit
.

ii)
f\equiv_{\omega}g
if and only if
f-g\in\mathcal{A}^{+}P_{k}+P_{k}^{-}(\omega)
.

Obviously, the relations
\equiv
and
\equiv_{\omega}
are equivalence ones. Denote by
QP_{k}(\omega)
the quotient of
P_{k}(\omega)
by the equivalence relation
\equiv_{\omega}
. Then, we have

QP_{k}(\omega)=P_{k}(\omega)/((\mathcal{A}^{+}P_{k}\cap P_{k}(\omega))+P_{k}^{-}(\omega)).

For a polynomial
f\in P_{k}
, we denote by
[f]
the class in
QP_{k}
represented by
f
. If
\omega
is a weight vector and
f\in P_{k}(\omega)
, then denote by
[f]_{\omega}
the class in
QP_{k}(\omega)
represented by
f
. Denote by
|S|
the cardinal of a set
S
.

From
[
25
]
, we have

(QP_{k})_{n}\cong\bigoplus_{\deg\omega=n}QP_{k}(\omega).

(2.1)

Definition 2.4
.

Let
x,y
be monomials of the same degree in
P_{k}
. We say that
x<y
if and only if one of the following holds:

i)
\omega(x)<\omega(y)
;

ii)
\omega(x)=\omega(y)
and
\sigma(x)<\sigma(y).

Definition 2.5
.

A monomial
x
in
P_{k}
is said to be inadmissible if there exist monomials
y_{1},y_{2},\ldots,y_{t}
such that
y_{j}<x
for
j=1,2,\ldots,t
and
x-\sum_{j=1}^{t}y_{j}\in\mathcal{A}^{+}P_{k}.

A monomial
x
is said to be admissible if it is not inadmissible.

Obviously, the set of all the admissible monomials of degree
n
in
P_{k}
is a minimal set of
\mathcal{A}
-generators for
P_{k}
in degree
n
.

Definition 2.6
.

A monomial
x
in
P_{k}
is said to be strictly inadmissible if and only if there exist monomials
y_{1},y_{2},\ldots,y_{t}
such that
y_{j}<x,
for
j=1,2,\ldots,t
and
x=\sum_{j=1}^{t}y_{j}+\sum_{u=1}^{2^{s}-1}Sq^{u}(h_{u})
with
s=\max\{i:\omega_{i}(x)>0\}
and suitable polynomials
h_{u}\in P_{k}
.

It is easy to see that if
x
is strictly inadmissible, then it is inadmissible.

Theorem 2.7

(See Kameko
[
4
]
, Sum
[
22
]
)
.

Let
x,y,w
be monomials in
P_{k}
such that
\omega_{i}(x)=0
for
i>r>0
,
\omega_{s}(w)\neq 0
and
\omega_{i}(w)=0
for
i>s>0
.

i)
If
w
is inadmissible, then
xw^{2^{r}}
is also inadmissible.

ii)
If
w
is strictly inadmissible, then
wy^{2^{s}}
is also strictly inadmissible.

Proposition 2.8

(See
[
24
]
)
.

Let
x
be an admissible monomial in
P_{k}
. Then we have

i)
If there is an index
i_{0}
such that
\omega_{i_{0}}(x)=0
, then
\omega_{i}(x)=0
for all
i>i_{0}
.

ii)
If there is an index
i_{0}
such that
\omega_{i_{0}}(x)<k
, then
\omega_{i}(x)<k
for all
i>i_{0}
.

Now, we recall a result of Singer
[
20
]
on the hit monomials in
P_{k}
.

Definition 2.9
.

A monomial
z
in
P_{k}
is called a spike if
\nu_{j}(z)=2^{d_{j}}-1
for
d_{j}
a non-negative integer and
j=1,2,\ldots,k
. If
z
is a spike with
d_{1}>d_{2}>\ldots>d_{r-1}\geqslant d_{r}>0
and
d_{j}=0
for
j>r,
then it is called the minimal spike.

In
[
20
]
, Singer showed that if
\mu(n)\leqslant k
, then there exists uniquely a minimal spike of degree
n
in
P_{k}
.
The following is a criterion for the hit monomials in
P_{k}
.

Theorem 2.10

(See Singer
[
20
]
)
.

Suppose
x\in P_{k}
is a monomial of degree
n
, where
\mu(n)\leqslant k
. Let
z
be the minimal spike of degree
n
. If
\omega(x)<\omega(z)
, then
x
is hit.

We need Silverman’s criterion for the hit polynomials in
P_{k}
.

Theorem 2.11

(See Silverman
[
17
, Theorem 1.2]
)
.

Let
p
be a polynomial of the form
fg^{2^{m}}
for some homogeneous polynomials
f
and
g
. If
\deg f<(2^{m}-1)\mu(\deg g)
, then
p
is hit.

This result leads to a criterion in terms of minimal spike which is a stronger version of Theorem
2.10
.

Theorem 2.12

(See Walker-Wood
[
30
, Theorem 14.1.3]
)
.

Let
x\in P_{k}
be a monomial of degree
n
, where
\mu(n)\leqslant k
and let
z
be the minimal spike of degree
n
. If there is an index
h
such that
\sum_{i=1}^{h}2^{i-1}\omega_{i}(x)<\sum_{i=1}^{h}2^{i-1}\omega_{i}(z),
then
x
is hit.

We set

\displaystyle P_{k}^{0}

\displaystyle=\langle\{x=x_{1}^{a_{1}}x_{2}^{a_{2}}\ldots x_{k}^{a_{k}}\ :\ a_{1}a_{2}\ldots a_{k}=0\}\rangle,

\displaystyle P_{k}^{+}

\displaystyle=\langle\{x=x_{1}^{a_{1}}x_{2}^{a_{2}}\ldots x_{k}^{a_{k}}\ :\ a_{1}a_{2}\ldots a_{k}>0\}\rangle.

It is easy to see that
P_{k}^{0}
and
P_{k}^{+}
are the
\mathcal{A}
-submodules of
P_{k}
. Furthermore, we have the following.

Proposition 2.13
.

We have a direct summand decomposition of the
\mathbb{F}_{2}
-vector spaces

QP_{k}=QP_{k}^{0}\oplus QP_{k}^{+}.

Here
QP_{k}^{0}=P_{k}^{0}/\mathcal{A}^{+}P_{k}^{0}
and
QP_{k}^{+}=P_{k}^{+}/\mathcal{A}^{+}P_{k}^{+}
.

For
1\leqslant i\leqslant k
, define the homomorphism
f_{i}:P_{k-1}\to P_{k}
of algebras by substituting

f_{i}(x_{j})=\begin{cases}x_{j},&\text{ if }1\leqslant j<i,\\
x_{j+1},&\text{ if }i\leqslant j<k.\end{cases}

(2.2)

Proposition 2.14

(See Mothebe and Uys
[
6
]
)
.

Let
i,d
be positive integers such that
1\leqslant i\leqslant k
. If
x
is an admissible monomial in
P_{k-1}
then
x_{i}^{2^{d}-1}f_{i}(x)
is also an admissible monomial in
P_{k}
.

Denote
\mathcal{N}_{k}=\{(i;I):I=(i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{r}),1\leqslant i<i_{1}<\ldots<i_{r}\leqslant k,\ 0\leqslant r<k\}
. For any
(i;I)\in\mathcal{N}_{k}
, we define the homomorphism
p_{(i;I)}:P_{k}\to P_{k-1}
of algebras by substituting

p_{(i;I)}(x_{j})=\begin{cases}x_{j},&\text{ if }1\leqslant j<i,\\
\sum_{s\in I}x_{s-1},&\text{ if }j=i,\\
x_{j-1},&\text{ if }i<j\leqslant k.\end{cases}

(2.3)

Then
p_{(i;I)}
is a homomorphism of
\mathcal{A}
-modules.

Lemma 2.15

(Phúc-Sum
[
12
]
)
.

If
x
is a monomial in
P_{k}
, then

p_{(i;I)}(x)\in P_{k-1}(\omega(x)).

This lemma implies that if
\omega
is a weight vector and
x\in P_{k}(\omega)
, then
p_{(i;I)}(x)\in P_{k-1}(\omega)
. Moreover,
p_{(i;I)}
passes to a homomorphism from
QP_{k}(\omega)
to
QP_{k-1}(\omega)
. So, these homomorphisms can be used to prove certain subset of
QP_{k}(\omega)
is linearly independent.

For
J=(j_{1},j_{2},\ldots,j_{r}):1\leqslant j_{1}<\ldots<j_{r}\leqslant k
, we define a monomorphism
\theta_{J}:P_{r}\to P_{k}
of
\mathcal{A}
-algebras by substituting

\theta_{J}(x_{t})=x_{j_{t}}\ \mbox{ for }\ 1\leqslant t\leqslant r.

(2.4)

It is easy to see that, for any weight vector
\omega
of degree
n
,

Q\theta_{J}(P_{r}^{+})(\omega)\cong QP_{r}^{+}(\omega)\mbox{ and }(Q\theta_{J}(P_{r}^{+}))_{n}\cong(QP_{r}^{+})_{n}

for
1\leqslant r\leqslant k
, where
Q\theta_{J}(P_{r}^{+})=\theta_{J}(P_{r}^{+})/\mathcal{A}^{+}\theta_{J}(P_{r}^{+})
. So, by a simple computation using Theorem
1.1
and (
2.1
), we get the following.

Proposition 2.16

(See Walker and Wood
[
30
]
)
.

For a weight vector
\omega
of degree
n
, we have direct summand decompositions of the
\mathbb{F}_{2}
-vector spaces

\displaystyle QP_{k}(\omega)

\displaystyle=\bigoplus_{\mu(n)\leqslant r\leqslant k}\bigoplus_{\ell(J)=r}Q\theta_{J}(P_{r}^{+})(\omega),

where
\ell(J)
is the length of
J
. Consequently

\displaystyle\dim QP_{k}(\omega)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mu(n)\leqslant r\leqslant k}{k\choose r}\dim QP_{r}^{+}(\omega),

\displaystyle\dim(QP_{k})_{n}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mu(n)\leqslant r\leqslant k}{k\choose r}\dim(QP_{r}^{+})_{n}.

Notation 2.17
.

From now on, we denote by
B_{k}(n)
the set of all admissible monomials of degree
n
in
P_{k}
,

B_{k}^{0}(n)=B_{k}(n)\cap P_{k}^{0},\ B_{k}^{+}(n)=B_{k}(n)\cap P_{k}^{+}.

For a weight vector
\omega
of degree
n
, we set

B_{k}(\omega)=B_{k}(n)\cap P_{k}(\omega),\ B_{k}^{+}(\omega)=B_{k}^{+}(n)\cap P_{k}(\omega).

For a subset
S\subset P_{k}
, we denote
[S]=\{[f]:f\in S\}
. If
S\subset P_{k}(\omega)
, then we set
[S]_{\omega}=\{[f]_{\omega}:f\in S\}
.
Then,
[B_{k}(\omega)]_{\omega}
and
[B_{k}^{+}(\omega)]_{\omega}
, are respectively the basses of the
\mathbb{F}_{2}
-vector spaces
QP_{k}(\omega)
and
QP_{k}^{+}(\omega):=QP_{k}(\omega)\cap QP_{k}^{+}
.

3.
Proof of Theorem
1.3

Firstly, we determine the weight vectors of the admissible monomials of degree
2^{d+1}-2
for
d\geqslant 5
.

Lemma 3.1
.

If
x
be an admissible monomial of degree
2^{d+1}-2
in
P_{5}
for
d\geqslant 5
, then either
\omega(x)=(2)|^{d}
or
\omega(x)=(4)|(3)|^{d-2}|(1)
.

We need the following lemma.

Lemma 3.2
.

Let
(i,j,t,u,v)
be an arbitrary permutation of
(1,2,3,4,5)
. The following monomials are strictly inadmissible:

i)

x_{i}^{2}x_{j}x_{t}^{3},\,i<j
;
x_{i}^{2}x_{j}x_{t}x_{u}^{2},\,i<j<t
;
x_{i}x_{j}^{2}x_{t}^{2}x_{u},i<j<t<u
.

ii)

x_{i}^{2}x_{j}^{2}x_{t}^{3}x_{u}^{3}
;
x_{i}^{2}x_{j}x_{t}^{2}x_{u}^{2}x_{v}^{3},\,
i ¡ j
;\ x_{i}^{2}x_{j}x_{t}x_{u}^{3}x_{v}^{3},\,i<j<t
.

iii)

x_{i}x_{j}^{2}x_{t}^{6}x_{u}^{6}x_{v}^{7}
;
x_{i}^{6}x_{j}^{3}x_{t}x_{u}^{6}x_{v}^{6},\,i<j
.

iv)

x_{i}^{3}x_{j}^{4}x_{t}^{4}x_{u}^{4}x_{v}^{7}
;
x_{i}^{3}x_{j}^{4}x_{t}^{4}x_{u}^{5}x_{v}^{6}
.

v)

x_{i}x_{j}^{3}x_{t}^{14}x_{u}^{14}x_{v}^{14}
,
x_{i}^{2}x_{j}^{3}x_{t}^{13}x_{u}^{14}x_{v}^{14}
,
x_{i}^{3}x_{j}^{5}x_{t}^{10}x_{u}^{14}x_{v}^{14}
,
x_{i}^{3}x_{j}^{6}x_{t}^{10}x_{u}^{13}x_{v}^{14}
.

Proof.

Parts i) and ii) are proved in Ly-Tín
[
5
]
. Parts ii) and iv) are prove in
[
26
]
. We prove Part v) for
x=x_{i}^{3}x_{j}^{5}x_{t}^{10}x_{u}^{14}x_{v}^{14}
. The others can be proved by the similar computations. By a direct computation, we have

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle=Sq^{1}\big(x_{i}^{3}x_{j}^{5}x_{t}^{10}x_{u}^{13}x_{v}^{14}+x_{i}^{3}x_{j}^{5}x_{t}^{12}x_{u}^{11}x_{v}^{14}+x_{i}^{5}x_{j}^{10}x_{t}^{10}x_{u}^{7}x_{v}^{13}+x_{i}^{5}x_{j}^{10}x_{t}^{12}x_{u}^{7}x_{v}^{11}

\displaystyle\quad+x_{i}^{5}x_{j}^{12}x_{t}^{10}x_{u}^{7}x_{v}^{11}+x_{i}^{9}x_{j}^{6}x_{t}^{10}x_{u}^{7}x_{v}^{13}+x_{i}^{10}x_{j}^{5}x_{t}^{12}x_{u}^{7}x_{v}^{11}+x_{i}^{12}x_{j}^{5}x_{t}^{10}x_{u}^{7}x_{v}^{11}\big)

\displaystyle\quad+Sq^{2}\big(x_{i}^{3}x_{j}^{6}x_{t}^{10}x_{u}^{11}x_{v}^{14}+x_{i}^{6}x_{j}^{10}x_{t}^{10}x_{u}^{7}x_{v}^{11}+x_{i}^{10}x_{j}^{6}x_{t}^{10}x_{u}^{7}x_{v}^{11}\big)

\displaystyle\hskip 136.5733pt+Sq^{4}\big(x_{i}^{5}x_{j}^{6}x_{t}^{10}x_{u}^{7}x_{v}^{14}\big)\ \mbox{mod}(P_{5}^{-}(2,4,3,3)).

The above equality shows that
x=x_{i}^{3}x_{j}^{5}x_{t}^{10}x_{u}^{14}x_{v}^{14}
is strictly inadmissible.
∎

Proof of Lemma
3.1
.

Observe that
z=x_{1}^{2^{d}-1}x_{2}^{2^{d}-1}
is the minimal spike of degree
2^{d+1}-2
in
P_{5}
and
\omega(z)=(2)|^{d}
. Since
2^{d+1}-2
is even, using Theorem
2.10
, we obtain
\omega_{1}(x)=2
or
\omega_{1}(x)=4
. If
\omega_{1}(x)=4
. Then
x=X_{i}y^{2}
with
y
an admissible monomial of degree
2^{d}-3
in
P_{5}
and
1\leqslant i\leqslant 5
. Combining Proposition
2.8
and a results in Phúc
[
9
]
we get
\omega(y)=(3)|^{d-2}|(1)
, so
\omega(x)=(4)|(3)|^{d-2}|(1)
.

Suppose
\omega_{1}(x)=2
. We prove
\omega(x)=(2)|^{d}
by induction on
d\geqslant 5
. Since
\omega_{1}(x)=2
,
x=x_{i}x_{j}y^{2}
with
1\leqslant i<j\leqslant 5
and
y
an admissible monomial of degree
2^{d}-1
. For
d=5
, in
[
26
]
we have proved that either
\omega(y)=(2)|^{4}
or
\omega(y)=(2,4,3,1)
or
\omega(y)=(4,3,3,1)
. By a direct computation we see that if
\omega(y)=(2,4,3,1)
, then there is a monomial
u
as given in one of Parts i) and iii) of Lemma
3.2
such that
x=uz^{2^{r}}
with
r=2,3
and
z
a suitable monomial. If
\omega(y)=(4,3,3,1)
, then there is a monomial
v
as given in one of Parts ii), iii) and v) of Lemma
3.2
such that
x=vh^{2^{r}}
with
r=2,3,4
and
h
a suitable monomial. By Theorem
2.7
,
x
is inadmissible. So, we get
\omega(y)=(2)|^{4}
and
\omega(x)=(2)|^{5}
. Suppose
d>5
, by the inductive hypothesis, we have
\omega(y)=(2)|^{d-1}
, hence
\omega(x)=(2)|^{d}
. The lemma is proved.
∎

By Lemma
3.1
, we have

(QP_{5})_{(2^{d+1}-2)}=(QP_{5}^{0})_{(2^{d+1}-2)}\oplus QP_{5}^{+}((2)|^{d})\oplus QP_{5}^{+}((4)|(3)|^{d-2}|(1)).

From Kameko
[
4
]
and our work
[
24
]
, we have

\displaystyle\dim(QP_{2}^{+})_{(2^{d+1}-2)}=\dim QP_{2}^{+}((2)|^{d})=1,

\displaystyle\dim(QP_{3}^{+})_{(2^{d+1}-2)}=\dim QP_{3}^{+}((2)|^{d})=4,

\displaystyle\dim QP_{4}^{+}((2)|^{d})=13,\ \dim QP_{4}^{+}((4)|(3)|^{d-2}|(1))=45

for any
d\geqslant 5
. Hence, we get

\displaystyle\dim QP_{5}^{0}((2)|^{d})={5\choose 2}+4{5\choose 3}+13{5\choose 4}=115,

\displaystyle\dim QP_{5}^{0}((4)|(3)|^{d-2}|(1))=45{5\choose 4}=225.

So, we need to determine the spaces
QP_{5}^{+}((2)|^{d})
and
QP_{5}^{+}((4)|(3)|^{d-2}|(1))
.

Proof of Proposition
3.1.1
.

Let
x
be a monomial of wight vector
(2)|^{d}
in

with

. By a direct computation we see that if

for all

, then there is a monomial

, and

suitable monomials. By Theorem

,

is inadmissible. Hence,

B_{5}^{+}((2)|^{d})\subset\{a_{d,t}:1\leqslant t\leqslant 40\}.

We now prove the set

is linearly independent in
QP_{5}((2)|^{d})\subset QP_{5}
. Suppose there is a linear relation

\mathcal{S}:=\sum_{1\leqslant t\leqslant 40}\gamma_{t}a_{d,t}\equiv 0,

(3.1)

where
\gamma_{t}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}
. We denote
\gamma_{\mathbb{J}}=\sum_{t\in\mathbb{J}}\gamma_{t}
for any

.

Let

, be as in Subsection

and the homomorphism

) for

. By using Lemma

. By applying

), we obtain

\displaystyle p_{(1;2)}(\mathcal{S})

\displaystyle\equiv\gamma_{\{7,9,11,13,14\}}u_{d,1}+\gamma_{8}u_{d,2}+\gamma_{9}u_{d,3}+\gamma_{10}u_{d,4}+\gamma_{11}u_{d,5}

\displaystyle\quad+\gamma_{13}u_{d,6}+\gamma_{14}u_{d,7}+\gamma_{16}u_{d,8}+\gamma_{\{5,9,11,14\}}u_{d,9}

\displaystyle\quad+\gamma_{\{6,13\}}u_{d,10}+\gamma_{12}u_{d,11}+\gamma_{15}u_{d,12}+\gamma_{25}u_{d,13}\equiv 0.

(3.2)

), we get

\displaystyle\equiv\gamma_{\{3,17,18,20,29\}}u_{d,1}+\gamma_{\{1,3,18,21,28,29\}}u_{d,5}

\displaystyle\quad+\gamma_{20}u_{d,10}+\gamma_{19}u_{d,11}+\gamma_{23}u_{d,12}+\gamma_{26}u_{d,13}\equiv 0.

(3.4)

The relation (

) implies

) and (

) we obtain

\displaystyle p_{(4;5)}(\mathcal{S})

\displaystyle\equiv\gamma_{4}u_{d,1}+\gamma_{24}u_{d,6}+\gamma_{33}u_{d,9}+\gamma_{34}u_{d,10}

\displaystyle\quad+\gamma_{37}u_{d,11}+\gamma_{38}u_{d,12}+\gamma_{39}u_{d,13}\equiv 0.

), (

) and (

\displaystyle p_{(2;3)}(\mathcal{S})

\displaystyle\equiv\gamma_{\{3,27,28,30,40\}}u_{d,1}+\gamma_{\{1,3,28,31,35,40\}}u_{d,5}

3.2.

\displaystyle\quad+x_{1}^{11}x_{2}^{21}x_{3}^{15}x_{4}^{18}x_{5}^{28}+x_{1}^{15}x_{2}^{12}x_{3}^{19}x_{4}^{19}x_{5}^{28}+x_{1}^{15}x_{2}^{13}x_{3}^{19}x_{4}^{18}x_{5}^{28}

\displaystyle\quad+x_{1}^{15}x_{2}^{15}x_{3}^{16}x_{4}^{19}x_{5}^{28}+Sq^{1}\big(x_{1}^{15}x_{2}^{15}x_{3}^{15}x_{4}^{19}x_{5}^{28}\big)+Sq^{2}\big(x_{1}^{15}x_{2}^{15}x_{3}^{15}x_{4}^{18}x_{5}^{28}\big)

\displaystyle\quad+Sq^{4}\big(x_{1}^{15}x_{2}^{12}x_{3}^{15}x_{4}^{19}x_{5}^{28}+x_{1}^{15}x_{2}^{13}x_{3}^{15}x_{4}^{18}x_{5}^{28}\big)+Sq^{8}\big(x_{1}^{8}x_{2}^{15}x_{3}^{15}x_{4}^{19}x_{5}^{28}

\displaystyle\quad+x_{1}^{9}x_{2}^{15}x_{3}^{15}x_{4}^{18}x_{5}^{28}+x_{1}^{11}x_{2}^{12}x_{3}^{15}x_{4}^{19}x_{5}^{28}+x_{1}^{11}x_{2}^{13}x_{3}^{15}x_{4}^{18}x_{5}^{28}\big)\ \mbox{mod}(P_{5}^{-}((3)|^{5})).

Hence, the monomial
z
is strictly inadmissible. The others can be proved by a similar computation.
∎

Proof of Proposition
3.2.1
.

Let
A(d)
and
C(d)
be as in Subsection
5.2
with
d\geqslant 5
and let
x\in P_{5}^{+}(\omega)
be an admissible monomial with
\omega:=(4)|(3)|^{d-1}
. By a direct computation we see that if
x\notin A(d)\cup C(d)
, then there is a monomial
w
as given in one of Lemmas
3.2.2
or
3.2.3
such that
x=yw^{2^{r}}z^{2^{r+u}}
with
r,\,u
nonnegative integers,
2\leqslant u\leqslant 5
, and
y,\,z
suitable monomials. By Theorem
2.7
,
x
is inadmissible. Hence,
B_{5}^{+}(\omega)\subset A(d)\cup C(d)
.

Now we prove that the set
[A(d)\cup C(d)]_{\omega}
is linearly independent in
QP_{5}(\omega)
.

Consider the subspaces
\langle[A(d)]_{\omega}\rangle\subset QP_{5}(\omega)
and
\langle[C(d)]_{\omega}\rangle\subset QP_{5}(\omega)
. It is easy to see that for any

, we have
x=f_{i}(y)
with

an admissible monomial of weight vector
(3)|(2)|^{d-1}
in
P_{4}
. By Proposition
2.14
,
x
is admissible. This implies
\dim\langle[A(d)]_{\omega}\rangle=160
. Since

for all

and
\nu(x)<2^{d}-1
for all

, we obtain
\langle[A(d)]_{\omega}\rangle\cap\langle[C(d)]_{\omega}\rangle=\{0\}
. Hence, we need only to prove the set
[C(d)]_{\omega}=\{[b_{d,t}]_{\omega}:1\leqslant t\leqslant 75\}
is linearly independent in
QP_{5}(\omega)
, where the monomials

, are determined as in Subsection
5.2
.

Suppose there is a linear relation

(3.13)

where

3.3.
Computation of
QP_{5}^{+}((4)|(3)|^{d-2}|(1))

For
1\leqslant i<j\leqslant 5
, denote

f_{(i,j)}=f_{i}f_{j-1}:P_{3}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\scriptstyle{f_{j-1}}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}P_{4}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\scriptstyle{f_{i}}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}P_{5}.

Here

and

are defined by (
2.2
).

Let
d\geqslant 6
. From Proposition
3.2.1
we see that for any
x\in B_{5}((4)|(3)|^{d-2})
there exists uniquely a pair
(i_{x},j_{x})
such that
1\leqslant i_{x}<j_{x}\leqslant 5
and
\nu_{i_{x}}(x)<16
,
\nu_{j_{x}}(x)<16
. The main result of this subsection is the following.

Proposition 3.3.1
.

For any
d\geqslant 6
, we have

B_{5}((4)|(3)|^{d-2}|(1))=\left\{xf_{(i_{x},j_{x})}\left(x_{t}^{2^{d-1}}\right):x\in B_{5}((4)|(3)|^{d-2}),\,t=1,\,2,\,3\right\}.

Consequently,

\dim QP_{5}((4)|(3)|^{d-2}|(1))=930,\ \dim(QP_{5})_{(2^{d+1}-2)}=155+930=1085.

Thus, Theorem
1.3
is proved. In the remaining part of the section, we prove Proposition
3.3.1
. We need the following.

Lemma 3.3.3
.

The monomials

\displaystyle\quad+x_{1}^{11}x_{2}^{21}x_{3}^{27}x_{4}^{29}x_{5}^{38}+x_{1}^{11}x_{2}^{21}x_{3}^{27}x_{4}^{30}x_{5}^{37}+x_{1}^{11}x_{2}^{21}x_{3}^{27}x_{4}^{37}x_{5}^{30}+x_{1}^{11}x_{2}^{21}x_{3}^{27}x_{4}^{38}x_{5}^{29}

\displaystyle\quad+x_{1}^{11}x_{2}^{21}x_{3}^{30}x_{4}^{27}x_{5}^{37}+x_{1}^{11}x_{2}^{21}x_{3}^{30}x_{4}^{35}x_{5}^{29}+x_{1}^{11}x_{2}^{21}x_{3}^{35}x_{4}^{29}x_{5}^{30}+x_{1}^{11}x_{2}^{21}x_{3}^{35}x_{4}^{30}x_{5}^{29}

\displaystyle\quad+x_{1}^{11}x_{2}^{21}x_{3}^{38}x_{4}^{27}x_{5}^{29}+x_{1}^{11}x_{2}^{23}x_{3}^{20}x_{4}^{27}x_{5}^{45}+x_{1}^{11}x_{2}^{23}x_{3}^{20}x_{4}^{43}x_{5}^{29}+x_{1}^{11}x_{2}^{23}x_{3}^{21}x_{4}^{26}x_{5}^{45}

\displaystyle\quad+x_{1}^{11}x_{2}^{23}x_{3}^{37}x_{4}^{26}x_{5}^{29}+x_{1}^{15}x_{2}^{15}x_{3}^{20}x_{4}^{27}x_{5}^{49}+x_{1}^{15}x_{2}^{15}x_{3}^{21}x_{4}^{26}x_{5}^{49}+x_{1}^{15}x_{2}^{17}x_{3}^{23}x_{4}^{26}x_{5}^{45}

\displaystyle\quad+x_{1}^{15}x_{2}^{23}x_{3}^{24}x_{4}^{27}x_{5}^{37}+x_{1}^{15}x_{2}^{23}x_{3}^{24}x_{4}^{35}x_{5}^{29}+x_{1}^{15}x_{2}^{23}x_{3}^{25}x_{4}^{28}x_{5}^{35}+x_{1}^{15}x_{2}^{23}x_{3}^{25}x_{4}^{34}x_{5}^{29}

\displaystyle\quad+x_{1}^{15}x_{2}^{23}x_{3}^{27}x_{4}^{28}x_{5}^{33}+Sq^{1}\big(x_{1}^{15}x_{2}^{23}x_{3}^{29}x_{4}^{29}x_{5}^{29}\big)+Sq^{2}\big(x_{1}^{15}x_{2}^{15}x_{3}^{23}x_{4}^{26}x_{5}^{45}

\displaystyle\quad+x_{1}^{15}x_{2}^{15}x_{3}^{23}x_{4}^{42}x_{5}^{29}+x_{1}^{15}x_{2}^{15}x_{3}^{39}x_{4}^{26}x_{5}^{29}+x_{1}^{15}x_{2}^{23}x_{3}^{23}x_{4}^{28}x_{5}^{35}+x_{1}^{15}x_{2}^{23}x_{3}^{23}x_{4}^{34}x_{5}^{29}

\displaystyle\quad+Sq^{4}\big(x_{1}^{15}x_{2}^{15}x_{3}^{20}x_{4}^{27}x_{5}^{45}+x_{1}^{15}x_{2}^{15}x_{3}^{20}x_{4}^{43}x_{5}^{29}+x_{1}^{15}x_{2}^{15}x_{3}^{21}x_{4}^{26}x_{5}^{45}

\displaystyle\quad+Sq^{8}\big(x_{1}^{9}x_{2}^{15}x_{3}^{23}x_{4}^{26}x_{5}^{45}+x_{1}^{9}x_{2}^{15}x_{3}^{23}x_{4}^{42}x_{5}^{29}+x_{1}^{9}x_{2}^{15}x_{3}^{39}x_{4}^{26}x_{5}^{29}+x_{1}^{9}x_{2}^{23}x_{3}^{27}x_{4}^{29}x_{5}^{30}

\displaystyle\quad+x_{1}^{11}x_{2}^{21}x_{3}^{30}x_{4}^{27}x_{5}^{29}+x_{1}^{23}x_{2}^{15}x_{3}^{23}x_{4}^{28}x_{5}^{29}+x_{1}^{23}x_{2}^{15}x_{3}^{24}x_{4}^{27}x_{5}^{29}\big)

\displaystyle\quad+Sq^{16}\big(x_{1}^{15}x_{2}^{15}x_{3}^{23}x_{4}^{28}x_{5}^{29}+x_{1}^{15}x_{2}^{15}x_{3}^{24}x_{4}^{27}x_{5}^{29}\big)\ \mbox{mod}(P_{5}^{-}((4)|(3)|^{4}|(1)).

Thus, the monomial
x
is strictly inadmissible.
∎

Proof of Proposition
3.3.1
.

Let
d\geqslant 6
and let
\tilde{x}\in P_{5}((4)|(3)|^{d-2}|(1))
be an admissible monomial. Then
\tilde{x}
is of the form
\tilde{x}=x(x_{r}^{2^{d-1}})
with
1\leqslant r\leqslant 5
and
x
a monomial of weight vector
(4)|(3)|^{d-2}
. From the proof of Proposition
3.2.1
we see that if
x\notin B_{5}\big((4)|(3)|^{d-2}\big)
, then
x
is strictly inadmissible, hence by Theorem
2.7
,
\tilde{x}
is strictly inadmissible. This implies a contradiction.

By a direct computation we see that if either
r=\nu_{i_{x}}(x)
or
r=\nu_{j_{x}}(x)
, then there is a monomial
w
as given in one of Lemmas
3.3.2
or
3.3.3
such that
x=zw^{2^{d-u}}
with
u
a nonnegative integer,
2\leqslant u\leqslant 6
, and
z
a monomial of weight vector
(4)|(3)|^{d-u}
. By Theorem
2.7
,
x
is inadmissible. So,
r\neq\nu_{i_{x}}(x)
,
r\neq\nu_{j_{x}}(x)
and
x_{r}^{2^{d-1}}=f_{(i_{x},j_{x})}\left(x_{t}^{2^{d-1}}\right)
with
t=1,\,2,\,3
. Hence, we obtain

B_{5}((4)|(3)|^{d-2}|(1))\subset\left\{xf_{(i_{x},j_{x})}\big(x_{t}^{2^{d-1}}\big):x\in B_{5}\big((4)|(3)|^{d-2}\big),\,t=1,\,2,\,3\right\}.

Now we prove that the set in the right hand side of the last relation is a minimal set of
\mathcal{A}
-generators for
P_{5}(\overline{\omega})
with
\overline{\omega}=(4)|(3)|^{d-2}|(1)
.

Suppose there is a linear relation

S:=\sum_{x\in B_{5}^{+}(\overline{\omega});\ t=1,2,3}\gamma_{x,t}\left(xf_{(i_{x},j_{x})}\big(x_{t}^{2^{d-1}}\big)\right)\equiv_{\overline{\omega}}0,

where
\gamma_{x,t}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}
.

Let
B_{4}(\overline{\omega})
be as in our work
[
24
]
and the homomorphism
p_{(i;I)}:P_{5}\to P_{4}
which is defined by (
2.3
) for
k=5
. Note that
|B_{4}(\overline{\omega})|=45
. From Lemma
2.15
, we see that
p_{(i;I)}
passes to a homomorphism from
QP_{5}(\overline{\omega})
to
QP_{4}(\overline{\omega})
. By a direct computation similar to the one in the proof of Proposition
3.2.1
, we compute
p_{(i;I)}(S))
in terms of the monomials in
B_{4}(\overline{\omega})
(mod(
\mathcal{A}^{+}P_{4}+P_{5}^{-}(\overline{\omega})
). Based on the relations
p_{(i;I)}(S))\equiv_{\overline{\omega}}0
with all
(i;I)\in\mathcal{N}_{5}
and
I\neq(2,3,4,5)
, we obtain
\gamma_{x,t}=0
for all
x\in B_{5}(\overline{\omega})
and
t=1,\,2,\,3.
The proof is completed.
∎

4.
Proof of Theorem
1.4

First of all, we determine the weight vectors of the admissible monomials of degree
2^{d+1}-1
for
d\geqslant 6
.

Lemma 4.1
.

Let
x
be an admissible monomial of degree
2^{d+1}-1
in
P_{5}
for
d\geqslant 6
. If
[x]\in\mbox{\rm Ker}\big((\widetilde{Sq}^{0}_{*})_{(5,2^{d}-3)}\big)
, then either
\omega(x)=(1)|^{d+1}
or
\omega(x)=(3)|(2)|^{d-1}
.

To prove the lemma, we need the following.

Lemma 4.2
.

Let
(i,j,t,u,v)
be an arbitrary permutation of
(1,2,3,4,5)
. The following monomials are strictly inadmissible:

i)

x_{i}^{2}x_{j}^{3}x_{t}^{3}x_{u}^{3}
;
x_{i}^{2}x_{j}x_{t}^{2}x_{u}^{3}x_{v}^{3},\,i<j
.

ii)

x_{i}x_{j}^{3}x_{t}^{14}x_{u}^{14}x_{v}^{15}
;
x_{i}^{3}x_{j}^{13}x_{t}^{2}x_{u}^{14}x_{v}^{15}
;
x_{i}^{3}x_{j}^{5}x_{t}^{10}x_{u}^{14}x_{v}^{15}
;
x_{i}^{3}x_{j}^{13}x_{t}^{6}x_{u}^{10}x_{v}^{15}
;
x_{i}^{3}x_{j}^{13}x_{t}^{14}x_{u}^{3}x_{v}^{14}
;
x_{i}^{3}x_{j}^{5}x_{t}^{14}x_{u}^{11}x_{v}^{14},\,t<u
;
x_{i}^{3}x_{j}^{13}x_{t}^{6}x_{u}^{11}x_{v}^{14}\,j<u
;
x_{i}^{3}x_{j}^{13}x_{t}^{7}x_{u}^{10}x_{v}^{14}\,j<t
.

iii)

x_{i}x_{j}^{7}x_{t}^{27}x_{u}^{30}x_{v}^{30}

x_{i}^{3}x_{j}^{5}x_{t}^{27}x_{u}^{30}x_{v}^{30}

x_{i}^{3}x_{j}^{7}x_{t}^{25}x_{u}^{30}x_{v}^{30}

x_{i}^{3}x_{j}^{7}x_{t}^{29}x_{u}^{26}x_{v}^{30}

x_{i}^{7}x_{j}^{11}x_{t}^{21}x_{u}^{26}x_{v}^{30}

x_{i}^{7}x_{j}^{11}x_{t}^{29}x_{u}^{22}x_{v}^{26}

x_{i}^{7}x_{j}^{27}x_{t}^{5}x_{u}^{26}x_{v}^{30}

x_{i}^{7}x_{j}^{27}x_{t}^{13}x_{u}^{22}x_{v}^{26}

x_{i}^{7}x_{j}^{27}x_{t}^{29}x_{u}^{2}x_{v}^{30}

x_{i}^{7}x_{j}^{27}x_{t}^{29}x_{u}^{6}x_{v}^{26}
.

Proof.

Part i) is easy. Part ii) is due to Phúc
[
10
]
. We prove Part iii) for the monomial
y=x_{i}^{3}x_{j}^{5}x_{t}^{27}x_{u}^{30}x_{v}^{30}
. The others can be proved by the similar computations. By a direct computation, we have

\displaystyle y

\displaystyle=Sq^{1}\big(x_{i}^{3}x_{j}^{9}x_{t}^{23}x_{u}^{29}x_{v}^{30}+x_{i}^{3}x_{j}^{12}x_{t}^{23}x_{u}^{27}x_{v}^{29}+x_{i}^{9}x_{j}^{18}x_{t}^{15}x_{u}^{23}x_{v}^{29}

\displaystyle\quad+x_{i}^{9}x_{j}^{20}x_{t}^{15}x_{u}^{23}x_{v}^{27}+x_{i}^{12}x_{j}^{17}x_{t}^{15}x_{u}^{23}x_{v}^{27}\big)+Sq^{2}\big(x_{i}^{3}x_{j}^{10}x_{t}^{23}x_{u}^{27}x_{v}^{30}

\displaystyle\quad+x_{i}^{10}x_{j}^{18}x_{t}^{15}x_{u}^{23}x_{v}^{27}\big)+Sq^{4}\big(x_{i}^{3}x_{j}^{5}x_{t}^{23}x_{u}^{30}x_{v}^{30}+x_{i}^{5}x_{j}^{18}x_{t}^{15}x_{u}^{23}x_{v}^{30}\big)

\displaystyle\quad+Sq^{8}\big(x_{i}^{5}x_{j}^{10}x_{t}^{15}x_{u}^{27}x_{v}^{30}\big)\ \mbox{mod}(P_{5}^{-}(3,4,3,3,3)).

Hence, the monomial
y
is strictly inadmissible.
∎

Proof of Lemma
4.1
.

Let
x
be an admissible monomial of degree
2^{d+1}-1
such that
[x]\in\mbox{\rm Ker}\big((\widetilde{Sq}^{0}_{*})_{(5,2^{d}-3)}\big)
. Since
2^{d+1}-1
is odd, we obtain either
\omega_{1}(x)=1
or
\omega_{1}(x)=3
or
\omega_{1}(x)=5
.

If
\omega_{1}(x)=5
, then
x=X_{\emptyset}y^{2}
with
y
an admissible monomial of degree
2^{d}-3
in
P_{5}
and
\widetilde{Sq}^{0}_{*})_{(5,2^{d}-3)}([x])=[y]\neq 0
. This contradicts the the hypothesis that
[x]\in\mbox{\rm Ker}\big((\widetilde{Sq}^{0}_{*})_{(5,2^{d}-3)}\big)
.

If
\omega_{1}(x)=1
, then
x=x_{i}y_{1}^{2}
with
y_{1}
an admissible monomial of degree
2^{d}-1
in
P_{5}
and
1\leqslant i\leqslant 5
. By Phúc
[
11
]
, we have
\omega(y_{1})=(1)|^{5}
, hence
\omega(x)=(1)|^{6}
for
d=5
. By induction on
d
we easily obtain
\omega(x)=(1)|^{d+1}
for any
d\geqslant 5
.

If
\omega_{1}(x)=3
, then we get
x=X_{i,j}y_{2}^{2}
with
1\leqslant i<j\leqslant 5
and
y_{2}
an admissible monomial of degree
2^{d}-2
. From Lemma
3.1
we have
\omega(y_{2})=(4)|(3)|^{d-3}|(1)
for any
d\geqslant 6
. By a direct computation we can see that there is a monomial
w
as given in Lemma
4.2
such that
X_{i,j}y_{2}^{2}=wh^{2^{r}}
with
2\leqslant r\leqslant 5
and
h
a suitable monomial. By Theorem
2.7
, the monomial
X_{i,j}y_{2}^{2}
is inadmissible. Thus, we get
\omega(y_{2})=(2)|^{d-1}
and
\omega(x)=(3)|(2)|^{d-1}
. The lemma is proved.
∎

By Lemma
4.1
, we have

\displaystyle\mbox{\rm Ker}\big((\widetilde{Sq}^{0}_{*})_{(5,2^{d}-3)}\big)

\displaystyle\cong QP_{5}\big((1)|^{d+1}\big)\oplus QP_{5}\big((3)|(2)|^{d-1}\big).

The space
QP_{5}\big((1)|^{d+1}\big)
has been determined in
[
24
]
. We have

Proposition 4.3

(See
[
24
, Proposition 4.1]
)
.

For any positive integer
s
, we have

B_{k}((1)|^{s})=\big\{x_{i_{1}}x_{i_{2}}^{2}\ldots x_{i_{m-1}}^{2^{m-2}}x_{i_{m}}^{2^{s}-2^{m-1}}:\ 1\leqslant i_{1}<\ldots<i_{m}\leqslant k;\,m\leqslant\min\{s,k\}\big\}.

This proposition implies
\dim QP_{5}\big((1)|^{d+1}\big)=31
for any
d\geqslant 4
. So, we need only to determine the space
QP_{5}\big((3)|(2)|^{d-1}\big)
.

From Kameko
[
4
]
and our work
[
24
]
, we have

|B_{3}^{+}\big((3)|(2)|^{d-1}\big)|=7,\ |B_{4}^{+}\big((3)|(2)|^{d-1}\big)|=42.

Hence, we obtain
|B_{5}^{0}\big((3)|(2)|^{d-1}\big)|=7\binom{5}{3}+42\binom{5}{4}=280
.

Proposition 4.4
.

Let
d
be a positive integer. If
d\geqslant 5
, then
QP_{5}^{+}\big((3)|(2)|^{d-1}\big)
is an
\mathbb{F}_{2}
-vector space of dimension
185
with a basis consisting of the classes represented by the admissible monomials which are determined as in Subsection
5.3
.
Consequently,
\dim QP_{5}\big((3)|(2)|^{d-1}\big)=465.

By combining Propositions
4.3
and
4.4
one gets Theorem
1.4
. In the remaining part of the section we prove Proposition
4.4
. We need some lemmas.

Lemma 4.5
.

Let
(i,j,t,u,v)
be an arbitrary permutation of
(1,2,3,4,5)
. The following monomials are strictly inadmissible:

i)
​
x_{i}^{2}x_{j}x_{t}x_{u}^{3},\,i<j<t
;

;

.

x_{i}^{3}x_{j}^{12}x_{t}x_{u}^{15}
,

,

,

;

,

,

, and

Proof of Proposition
4.4
.

Let
d\geqslant 5
and let
x\in P_{5}^{+}((3)|(2)|^{d-1})
be an admissible monomial. Then
x=X_{i,j}y^{2}
with
1\leqslant i<j\leqslant 5
, and
y
a monomial of weight vector
(2)|^{d-1}
. By Theorem
2.7
, we have
y\in B_{5}((2)^{d-1})
.

Let
z\in B_{5}((2)^{d-1})
such that
X_{i,j}z^{2}\in P_{5}^{+}
and
\widetilde{A}(d)
,
\widetilde{C}(d)
as defined in Subsection
5.3
. By a direct computation we see that if
X_{i,j}z^{2}\notin\widetilde{A}(d)\cup\widetilde{C}(d)
, then there is a monomial
w
as given in one of Lemmas
4.5
and
4.6
such that
X_{i,j}z^{2}=wh^{2^{s}}
with
s
a nonnegative integer,
2\leqslant s\leqslant 5
, and
h
a monomial of weight vector
(2)|^{d-s}
. By Theorem
2.7
,
X_{i,j}z^{2}
is inadmissible. Since
x=X_{i,j}y^{2}
is admissible and
y\in B_{5}((2)^{d-1})
, we obtain
x\in\widetilde{A}(d)\cup\widetilde{C}(d)
. This implies that

B_{5}((3)|(2)|^{d-1})\subset\widetilde{A}(d)\cup\widetilde{C}(d).

We prove that
\widetilde{A}(d)\cup\widetilde{C}(d)
is a minimal set of
\mathcal{A}
-generators for
P_{5}^{+}(\tilde{\omega})
with
\tilde{\omega}:=(3)|(2)|^{d-1}
.

Consider the subspaces
\langle[\widetilde{A}(d)]_{\tilde{\omega}}\rangle\subset QP_{5}(\tilde{\omega})
and
\langle[\widetilde{C}(d)]_{\tilde{\omega}}\rangle\subset QP_{5}(\tilde{\omega})
. Observe that for any

, we have

with

an admissible monomial of weight vector
(2)|(1)|^{d-1}
in
P_{4}
. By Proposition
2.14
,
x
is admissible and we get
\dim\langle[\widetilde{A}(d)]_{\tilde{\omega}}\rangle=35
. Since
\nu(x)=2^{d}-1
for all

and
\nu(x)<2^{d}-1
for all
x\in\widetilde{C}(d)
, we obtain
\langle[\widetilde{A}(d)]_{\tilde{\omega}}\rangle\cap\langle[\widetilde{C}(d)]_{\tilde{\omega}}\rangle=\{0\}
. So, we need only to prove the set

is linearly independent in

, where the monomials

, are determined as in Subsection
5.3
.

By a direct computation one gets the following.

Lemma 5.4.1
.

The following monomials are strictly inadmissible:

x_{1}^{3}x_{2}^{7}x_{3}^{8}x_{4}^{29}x_{5}^{15}

x_{1}^{3}x_{2}^{7}x_{3}^{24}x_{4}^{13}x_{5}^{15}

Based on Lemmas
3.2.2
,
5.4.1
and Theorem
2.7
we can show that if a monomial
y\in B_{5}((3)|^{3}|(1))
, such that
X_{i}y^{2}\in P_{5}^{+}
and
X_{i}y^{2}\notin A(5)\cup B(5)\cup C(5)\cup D(5)
, then
X_{i}y^{2}
is inadmissible, where
1\leqslant i\leqslant 5
. This implies

B_{5}^{+}((4)|(3)|^{3}|(1))\subset A(5)\cup B(5)\cup C(5)\cup D(5).

Hence, we obtain

\displaystyle\dim QP_{5}^{+}((4)|(3)|^{3}|(1))\leqslant 230+270+295=795,

\displaystyle\dim(QP_{5})_{62}\leqslant 155+225+795=1175.

Furthermore, we can give a lower bound for
\dim(QP_{5})_{62}
as follows.

Set
\omega^{*}:=(4)|(3)|^{3}|(1)
and consider the subspaces

\langle[A(5)]_{\omega^{*}}\rangle\subset QP_{5}(\omega^{*}),\ \langle[B(5)\cup C(d)]_{\omega^{*}}\rangle\subset QP_{5}(\omega^{*}).

Observe that for any
x\in A(5)
, we have
x=x_{i}^{31}f_{i}(y)
with
y
an admissible monomial of weight vector
(3)|(2)|^{3}
in
P_{4}
. By Proposition
2.14
,
x
is admissible and we get
\dim\langle[A(5)]_{\omega^{*}}\rangle=230
. Since
\nu(x)=31
for all
x\in A(5)
and
\nu(x)<31
for all
x\in B(5)\cup C(5)
, we obtain
\langle[A(5)]_{\omega^{*}}\rangle\cap\langle[B(5)\cup C(5)]_{\omega^{*}}\rangle=\{0\}
. By a direct computation we get the following.

Proposition 5.4.2
.

The set
[B(5)\cup C(5)]_{\omega^{*}}
is linearly independent in the vector space
QP_{5}(\omega^{*})
.

So, we obtain a lower bound

\dim(QP_{5})_{62}\geqslant 155+225+725=1105.

5.5.
A generating set for
(QP_{5})_{63}

By a similar argument as in the proof of Lemma
4.1
we have

Lemma 5.5.1
.

Let
x
be an admissible monomial of degree
63
in
P_{5}
such that
[x]\in\mbox{\rm Ker}\big((\widetilde{Sq}^{0}_{*})_{(5,29)}\big)
. Then, either
\omega(x)=(1)|^{6}
or
\omega(x)=(3)|(2)|^{4}
or
\omega(x)=(3,4,3,3,1)
.

By Propositions
4.3
and
4.4
, we have
\dim QP_{5}((1)|^{6})=31
,
\dim QP_{5}((3)|(2)|^{4})=465.

We need to determine
QP_{5}(3,4,3,3,1).

.

(i,j,t,u,v)

(1,2,3,4,5)
. The following monomials are strictly inadmissible:

Based on Lemmas
4.2
and

Proposition 5.5.3
.

We denote

\displaystyle p

\displaystyle=x_{1}x_{2}x_{3}^{3}x_{4}^{30}x_{5}^{28}+x_{1}x_{2}x_{3}^{7}x_{4}^{26}x_{5}^{28}+x_{1}x_{2}^{3}x_{3}^{6}x_{4}^{25}x_{5}^{28}+x_{1}x_{2}^{3}x_{3}^{7}x_{4}^{24}x_{5}^{28}
